# [Feat] add rmsnorm fp4 quant fusion of flashinfer

[Upstream PR](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413) · state: open · merged: False

## Original PR description

## Purpose
This commit implements rmsnorm + fp4 quant fusion using FlashInfer, and integrate to rmsnorm + quant fusion pass, fixing https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/32612

## Test Plan
The tests are divided into 4 groups across two sets of execution commands:

- Main branch: Default parameters
- Main branch: Forced rmsnorm fusion enabled
- Current PR: Default parameters
- Current PR: Forced rmsnorm fusion enabled

```
# default
vllm serve nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4
# fused
vllm serve nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 --compilation-config '{"custom_ops":["-rms_norm"], "pass_config":{"fuse_norm_quant":true}}'
```

## Review and discussion

### reviews

- gemini-code-assist[bot] · COMMENTED · 2026-03-08T17:35:57Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#pullrequestreview-3911802918)

## Code Review

This pull request introduces a fusion pass for RMSNorm and FP4 quantization, leveraging FlashInfer kernels. The changes are well-structured, including new custom ops, updates to the fusion pass logic, configuration adjustments, and a comprehensive test case. The implementation is sound, but I've found a potential issue regarding the shapes of dummy tensors used for pattern matching, which could impact the correctness of the fusion pass.

 <sub>**Note:** Security Review is unavailable for this PR.</sub>

As shown in the images, before enabling the fusion, the operations are split between a complex Triton reduction kernel (`triton_red_fused_to_copy_add_embedding_mean_mul_pow_rsqrt_scaled_fp4_quant_0`) and a quant kernel(`vllm::cvt_fp16_to_fp4`). After enabling it, they are replaced by an embedding kernel (triton_poi_fused__to_copy_embedding_0) and the flashinfer-based RMSNormFP4QuantKernel. We still have two kernels and haven't reduced the total launch count, I wonder if this is an issue.
These benchmarks already include the `fused_add_rms_norm + fp4 fusion`.

- sparkecho · comment · 2026-03-16T03:23:21Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#issuecomment-4064812622)

How I collected and compared the benchmark data:
First, I used the shell script below to gather raw metrics. For each metric, I ran the test three times and selected the median value to mitigate outliers. Finally, I used AI to perform an initial comparison and double-check the results, followed by a thorough manual review to ensure accuracy.
## vllm serve
```bash
# dense
vllm serve nvidia/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-NVFP4
# moe
vllm serve nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4
# dense fused
vllm serve nvidia/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-NVFP4 --compilation-config '{"custom_ops":["-rms_norm"], "pass_config":{"fuse_norm_quant":true}}'
# moe fused
vllm serve nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 --compilation-config '{"custom_ops":["-rms_norm"], "pass_config":{"fuse_norm_quant":true}}'
```
```bash
for i in $(seq 3); do guidellm benchmark --target "http://localhost:8000/v1" --profile concurrent --rate 10 --data "prompt_tokens=64,output_tokens=128" --max-seconds 30 |& grep -A 7 'Server Throughput Statistics (All Requests)' ; done

for i in $(seq 3); do vllm bench serve --input-len 100 --output-len 100 --num-prompts 512 |& grep -A 23 'Serving Benchmark Result'; done

for i in $(seq 3); do python tests/evals/gsm8k/gsm8k_eval.py --port 8000 |& grep -A 6 'Results:'; done
```

## latency
```bash
echo
echo 'nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4'
for INPUT_LEN in 512 2048 8192; do
  for i in $(seq 3); do
    echo input_len: $INPUT_LEN
    vllm bench latency --input-len $INPUT_LEN --output-len 1 --batch-size 1 \
      --model nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 |& \
    grep -A 6 'Avg latency:'
  done
done

echo
echo 'nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 (fused)'
for INPUT_LEN in 512 2048 8192; do
  for i in $(seq 3); do
    echo input_len: $INPUT_LEN
    vllm bench latency --input-len $INPUT_LEN --output-len 1 --batch-size 1 \
      --model nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 \
      --compilation-config '{"custom_ops":["-rms_norm"], "pass_config":{"fuse_norm_quant":true}}' |& \
    grep -A 6 'Avg latency:'
  done
done

echo
echo 'nvidia/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-NVFP4'
for INPUT_LEN in 512 2048 8192; do
  for i in $(seq 3); do
    echo input_len: $INPUT_LEN
    vllm bench latency --input-len $INPUT_LEN --output-len 1 --batch-size 1 \
      --model nvidia/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-NVFP4 |& \
    grep -A 6 'Avg latency:'
  done
done

echo
echo 'nvidia/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-NVFP4 (fused)'
for INPUT_LEN in 512 2048 8192; do
  for i in $(seq 3); do
    echo input_len: $INPUT_LEN
    vllm bench latency --input-len $INPUT_LEN --output-len 1 --batch-size 1 \
      --model nvidia/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-NVFP4 \
      --compilation-config '{"custom_ops":["-rms_norm"], "pass_config":{"fuse_norm_quant":true}}' |& \
    grep -A 6 'Avg latency:'
  done
done
```

How do you want to proceed here? We could merge this fusion first and just not enable it by default. And then work on the custom fused kernel and merge that one, to see if we can speed that one up more than the flashinfer one. Or we could just go work on the custom fused kernel again. Or we can ask FI team if they can improve their kernel (might take longer). Up to you!

- sparkecho · comment · 2026-03-18T04:47:05Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#issuecomment-4079660525)

I agree with the first approach—let’s merge the FlashInfer integration now but keep it disabled by default. The implementation is straightforward and looks solid, so getting it into the codebase should be helpful for other developers who want to experiment with the operator in the meantime.

Regarding the performance: from a micro-benchmark perspective, the CUDA custom kernel really should be yielding more significant gains. It’s likely that there’s a subtle bottleneck or an implementation detail I haven't quite nailed yet. I’m planning to do a deep dive to figure out exactly where the performance is leaking; having the FlashInfer version merged will actually make it easier for me to run side-by-side comparisons.

To keep the PR as clean as possible for this initial merge, which way would you prefer I handle the "disabled" state?

1. Comment out the activation/registration code (modifications to `RMSNormQuantFusionPass` in `rms_quant_fusion.py`, `fix_functionalization.py`, and `enable_norm_fusion` in `vllm.py`).

2. Remove the activation code entirely for this PR, keeping only the core implementation.

3. Only keep the changes in `flashinfer.py` that introduce the operator, leaving the fusion pass and integration logic for a subsequent PR once the custom kernel is ready.

After the update, I'll rerun the benchmarks on B200 to ensure no side effects are introduced.

Looking forward to your thoughts!

- sparkecho · comment · 2026-04-19T17:22:22Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#issuecomment-4276424104)

Based on the nsys profile, the RMSNorm FP4 fused kernel has been correctly dispatched. However, looking at the performance data, the execution time of the fused kernel is currently not lower than the combined time of the two original kernels. I am still deep-diving into the analysis to identify the bottleneck.

Any insights or suggestions on this would be greatly appreciated!

Based on previous microbenchmark data, the fused kernel performs poorly when the input size is (1,2048). While this aligns with the 2048 hidden size of the nvidia/Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 model, it doesn't fully explain the latency variances across different input lengths. I will run an additional set of microbenchmarks to help narrow down the cause.

- namgyu-yun · comment · 2026-07-06T07:51:12Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#issuecomment-4890375023)

@sparkecho @ProExpertProg — I've been following this work and ran the profiling this thread has been waiting on.
> setup: NVIDIA Blackwell (RTX PRO 6000 / SM120, FlashInfer 0.6.13)

Full logs + traces: [micro/latency logs (1.6 MB)](https://github.com/namgyu-youn/vllm/releases/download/pr36413-profiling-2026-07-06/pr36413_logs.tar.gz), [Qwen traces (22 MB)](https://github.com/namgyu-youn/vllm/releases/download/pr36413-profiling-2026-07-06/pr36413_traces_qwen.tar.gz), [Llama traces (15 MB)](https://github.com/namgyu-youn/vllm/releases/download/pr36413-profiling-2026-07-06/pr36413_traces_llama.tar.gz). Hope to being answers the questions the review stalled on!

---

> do we have any microbenchmark results that show that the fused kernel is slower than unfused? Then we can submit an issue to the flashinfer team

No — the FlashInfer kernel is not the problem. Against the truly unfused pair the CuTe-DSL kernel wins 3.1–12.5x from 512 to 8192 tokens (the in-tree sweep stops at 1024, below the disputed regime; extended sweep in `micro.log` above).

> the execution time of the fused kernel is currently not lower than the combined time of the two original kernels

The traces explain this: **the baseline is not unfused**. Inductor already compiles `fused_add_rms_norm + scaled_fp4_quant` into one Triton kernel, so the fused op's real competitor is that kernel — and against it, CuTe-DSL wins prefill (23.8 vs 37.4 us/launch, −36%) but loses decode (2.46 vs 1.64 us/launch, +50%) on Qwen3-30B-A3B at 2048, launch-count matched.

**Why MoE regresses while dense improves:** when the pass consumes the input-norm site, inductor regroups and the post-attention-norm + MoE-prologue Triton kernel (`triton_red_fused_fused_add_rms_norm_moe_forward_1`) grows 1.36 → 2.21 ms (+63%, same launch count) — outweighing the CuTe-DSL win. On dense Llama both norm sites are replaced cleanly with no neighboring-kernel change:

| `vllm bench latency` (out=1, bs=1), baseline → fused | 512 | 2048 | 8192 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct-FP4 (dense) | −0.7% | −0.1% | −2.2% |
| Qwen3-30B-A3B-NVFP4 (MoE) | +1.6% | +2.0% | ±0% |

So the default-off gate question isn't "is the kernel fast" (it is) — it's whether the pattern's interaction with inductor grouping can be fixed for MoE, e.g. by also covering the MoE-side norm site.

Method notes: single run per cell (10 iters), but the arms' p10–p90 bands don't overlap at 512/2048 and the trace-level deltas corroborate the direction; clocks not locked. Hardware is SM120/GDDR7, not B200 — absolute numbers may shift, structural findings should transfer. Happy to share the rebased branch (includes the `MatcherFusedAddRMSNorm` → `vllm.ir.ops` port the rebase needs anyway).

*Disclosure: profiling and analysis done with AI assistance (Claude); I reviewed and ran everything.*

- gemini-code-assist[bot] · comment · 2026-03-08T17:35:57Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#discussion_r2902168217)

File: `vllm/compilation/passes/fusion/rms_quant_fusion.py` · line None

![high](https://www.gstatic.com/codereviewagent/high-priority.svg)

The shape of the `output_scale` dummy tensor appears to be incorrect. For an input of shape `(5, 16)`, the `scaled_fp4_quant` operation is expected to produce an `output_scale` of shape `(128, 1)`, but it's currently hardcoded to `(128, 4)`. This mismatch could lead to issues during graph tracing for pattern matching.

The shape calculation is as follows:
- `m=5, n=16, block_size=16`
- `rounded_m = round_up(5, 128) = 128`
- `scale_n = n // block_size = 16 // 16 = 1`
- `rounded_n = round_up(1, 4) = 4`
- `output_scale` shape should be `(rounded_m, rounded_n // 4) = (128, 1)`

```suggestion
        output_scale = torch.empty(128, 1, dtype=torch.int32, device=self.device)
```

- gemini-code-assist[bot] · comment · 2026-03-08T17:35:57Z · [source](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36413#discussion_r2902168221)

This could cause problems with pattern matching.

```
